### Pair 263
- **question**: What is the common sense law of inertia and how does it relate to the frame problem?
- **gold**: The common sense law of inertia is a default assumption that an action will not change a given property of a situation unless there is explicit evidence to the contrary. Rather than having to explicitly state every property that remains unchanged after an action, this principle allows us to assume stability by default. The frame problem, in its technical sense, is the challenge of formally expressing this intuitive law within a logical system—that is, finding a way to capture and encode this commonsense reasoning about what actions do not affect in a mathematically rigorous way.
- **model answer**:

  The common sense law of inertia is a general rule-of-thumb that an action can be assumed not to change a given property of a situation unless there is evidence to the contrary. This default assumption, or "default," captures the intuitive notion that actions typically do not affect properties they are not explicitly designed to change. The frame problem in AI and KR (knowledge representation) literature arises because specifying all effects and non-effects of an action is extremely difficult—much harder than identifying what changes when performing an action—and this difficulty extends even to preconditions, which must be specified for successful execution of the action.

### Pair 264

### Pair 266
- **question**: What structural pattern do arguments from ignorance follow in debates about self-locating belief?
- **gold**: Arguments from ignorance follow a three-step structure: first, a case is constructed in which a subject is stipulated to possess all knowledge of a particular type (K-type knowledge); second, an intuition is elicited that the subject nonetheless displays a specific form of ignorance; and third, a conclusion is drawn that not all knowledge can be reduced to that type K. This pattern mirrors the knowledge argument against materialism in philosophy of mind, as exemplified by Jackson's work. The messy shopper case demonstrates this approach by showing that Perry, before his epiphany, already held all relevant de re and descriptive beliefs yet still had something crucial to learn, suggesting the existence of a distinctively self-locating or indexical kind of belief distinct from traditional propositional belief.
- **model answer**:

  Arguments from ignorance follow the same general form as arguments against materialism in philosophy of mind, which were developed by Jackson (1982, 1986). The core structure involves stipulating that a subject possesses all relevant types of knowledge and then demonstrating that this subject still exhibits a particular kind of ignorance. From there, the conclusion drawn is that not all knowledge falls under the category of type K—meaning some forms of belief must be fundamentally different from traditional or descriptive beliefs. In the messy shopper case example, Perry's epiphany does not change his existing de re and descriptive beliefs about who owns the sack containing the messy shopper; however, this demonstrates he possesses a distinctively self-locating kind of belief that cannot be reduced to either type K knowledge alone.

### Pair 268
- **question**: Why can't conclusions about infinitesimal uncertainty dynamics be reliably applied to finite uncertainties?
- **gold**: The derivation of global Lyapunov exponents relies on linearized dynamics that are only valid for infinitesimal uncertainties. When uncertainties are finite, these linearized dynamics do not apply, so no valid conclusions about finite uncertainty behavior can be drawn from the infinitesimal case. Additionally, infinitesimal uncertainties do not become finite in finite time under normal conditions, and even if they did, this would require assuming the dynamics is unconfined—an assumption that contradicts the typical focus of nonlinear dynamics on interesting features that occur in bounded subregions of state space.
- **model answer**:

  Finite uncertainties cannot be reliably treated as infinitesimal because the dynamics used to derive Lyapunov exponents and other measures of chaotic behavior presuppose infinitesimal uncertainties, which do not actually occur in finite uncertainty scenarios. Since finite uncertainties never become infinitesimal in finite time (unless superexponential growth is involved), any conclusions drawn from these dynamics would be invalid for real-world applications where uncertainties are always bounded. Additionally, the standard account fails to address how to define appropriate infinitesimal transition probabilities that correctly generate quantum-mechanical probabilities at each step—this requires specifying a particular class of such probabilities rather than assuming they automatically exist.
